國際經濟金融週報內容摘要 
美 
國 
美國經濟維持溫和擴張，勞動市場降溫與通膨黏著牽動聯準會政策 
經濟 
⚫ 2026 年第 2 季美國實質 GDP 季增年率終值上修至 2.2%， 惟低於第1 季
之 2.5%。成長主要由個人消費及固定投資帶動，其中消費貢獻 2.51 個
百分點；惟進口大增 12.6%，致淨出口拖累 1.10 個百分點。另實質私人
國內最終銷售成長 4.6%，顯示內需仍具韌性。 
⚫ 9 月美國非農就業僅增 2.9 萬人，遠低於前月及近一年平均，失業率升
至 4.2%， 薪資增幅續降 ， 顯示企業聘僱動能轉弱 。 惟勞動市場尚未明顯
惡化，後續仍須關注就業降溫對聯準會利率決策之影響。 
⚫ 8 月美國職位空缺數由 733.5 萬個降至 707.9 萬個，職缺率降至 4.3%，
顯示企業徵才需求略為降溫；惟聘僱人數增至 519.2 萬人。主動離職及
裁員規模同步下降，反映勞工轉職意願與企業人力調整趨於審慎，勞動
市場供需大致維持平衡。 
⚫ 9 月美國 ISM 製造業採購經理人指數為 54.5，連續第九個月維持擴張。
新訂單、就業及未完成訂單同步改善，顯示需求仍具韌性；惟生產、出
口及進口動能略為放緩，且原物料成本持續攀升，加上關稅政策、地緣
政治及供應鏈不確定性仍存，製造業仍面臨部分壓力。 
⚫ 8 月美國 PCE 物價指數月增 0.3%、年增 3.4%，核心 PCE 月增 0.2%、
年增 3.0%， 顯示通膨大致持穩但短期物價壓力略升 。 另一方面 ， 個人消
費支出月增 0.9%、實質消費成長 0.6%，帶動儲蓄率降至 4.1%，顯示家
庭需求仍具韌性；後續須關注消費熱度及通膨對聯準會政策之影響。 
重要 
事件 
⚫ 近期聯準會官員立場趨於分歧，理事 Michael Barr 認為能源價格及 AI
投資熱潮可能延緩通膨降溫 ， 未來仍可能進一步升息 ； 惟John Williams、
Philip Jefferson 及 Michelle Bowman 等官員則主張暫無急於升息的必要 ，
傾向觀察後續經濟數據。加以 9 月非農就業明顯放緩，市場對 10 月升
息預期大幅降溫，焦點轉向 12 月會議。 
⚫ 台積電傳評估與馬斯克旗下 Terafab 合作赴德州設廠，雙方仍處討論階
段。合作可能由台積電主導營運，或由 Terafab 持多數股權、台積電提
供技術 。 若成真 ， 將擴大台積電美國布局 ， 並支援Tesla、SpaceX 及 xAI
晶片需求。

短評 
⚫ 美國經濟目前仍維持溫和擴張，內需與投資表現優於預期，短期衰退風
險有限 ； 惟近期新增就業明顯低於預期 ， 企業聘僱意願與職缺需求放緩 ，
勞動市場動能已有轉弱跡象，而通膨受能源價格、 AI 投資及關稅等因
素影響仍具黏著性。在經濟成長放緩與通膨尚未完全受控的環境下，聯
準會短期可能傾向維持觀望，惟若通膨再度升溫，不排除年底前仍有進
一步升息空間。 
歐 
元 
區 
法國殖利率大幅上升，不利於投資及消費表現 
經濟 
⚫ 歐元區 9 月 HICP 通膨、核心通膨(不含食品及能源)分別上升 0.6、0.1
個百分點至 3.8%、2.5%。 
⚫ 歐元區 8 月失業率持平 6.4%。 
⚫ 歐元區第二季家庭儲蓄率下降 0.2 個百分點至 14.2%。 
⚫ 歐元區第二季房價年增率為 4%，較第一季降 0.6 個百分點。 
重要 
事件 
⚫ 法國 10 年期公債殖利率升至 4.9%以上，與德國公債殖利率差距擴大至
125 個基點以上。 
短評 ⚫ 法國財政前景不明朗 ， 未來仍可能面臨殖利率進一步上升與財政壓力加劇的風險，暫時不利於消費及投資表現。 
日 
本 
景氣溫和復甦，但消費放緩與通膨升溫風險升高 
經濟 
⚫ 8 月工業生產月減 1.7%。 
⚫ 8 月零售月減 1.2%。 
⚫ 9 月東京核心通膨年增 2.7%。 
短評 
⚫ 儘管消費動能放緩與通膨壓力升溫 ， 但企業投資與生產回升趨勢仍具韌
性，經濟可望維持溫和復甦，惟日本央行進一步升息的可能性正逐步提
高。 
中 
國 
為刺激內需、引導產業走向，人行漸近式放寬政策工具 
經濟 ⚫ 9 月官方製造業 PMI、非製造業 PMI 分別上升為 50.1、50.2。 
重要 
事件 
⚫ 人行於 9 月 29 日宣布下調抵押補充貸款(該貸款本質是將資金引導至政
府希望發展的領域)利率自 1.75%降至 1.50%，並擴大適用範圍。 
短評 
⚫ 未來若內需復甦力道不如預期 ， 不排除人行將進一步降準或擴大結構性
貨幣工具支持經濟 ； 至於全面降息則仍須兼顧人民幣匯率及中美利差等
因素，可能性相對較低。

國際經濟金融週報 
兆豐國際商業銀行 徵信處 
115.10.5 
一、 美國—美國經濟維持溫和擴張，勞動市場降溫與通膨黏著牽動聯準會政策 
2026 年第二季美國實質 GDP 季增年率終值由前次估計之 1.5%上修至 2.2%，
惟仍低於第一季之 2.5%， 顯示經濟持續擴張但成長動能略有放緩 。 此次上修主要
反映投資、消費及政府支出優於原先估計。就成長結構觀察，個人消費支出仍為
主要動能，貢獻 2.51 個百分點，其中服務及商品消費分別貢獻 1.57 及 0.94 個百
分點；私人投資貢獻 0.82 個百分點，以設備投資及智慧財產權投資表現強勁，惟
庫存調整拖累 0.53 個百分點。對外貿易方面，出口成長 5.0%，但進口大幅增加
12.6%， 致淨出口拖累GDP 成長 1.10 個百分點 。 另反映內需基本面的實質私人國
內最終銷售成長 4.6%， 高於第一季之1.8%， 顯示民間需求明顯增強 。 物價方面 ，
第 2 季 PCE 及核心 PCE 物價指數分別上升 5.0%及 3.3%， 顯示通膨壓力仍偏高 。
整體而言，美國經濟仍由消費與固定投資支撐，惟進口快速增加及通膨黏著性，
仍為後續經濟前景的重要觀察重點。 
2026 年 9 月美國非農就業人數增加 2.9 萬人 ， 明顯低於上月修正後的13.3 萬
人，也低於過去 12 個月平均每月增加 4.5 萬人的水準；失業率小幅升至 4.2%，
整體仍維持在 3 月以來 4.1%至 4.3%的區間，反映勞動市場整體變化有限，惟新
增就業力道已轉趨疲弱。家庭調查方面，勞動參與率升至 61.8%，就業人口比為
59.2%，均較上月小幅上升；長期失業人數約 194.4 萬人，占總失業人口 27.1%，
大致維持穩定。產業別方面，主要產業就業變化有限，其中醫療保健增加 1.7 萬
人、營建業增加 1.1 萬人、製造業增加 0.9 萬人，金融活動則減少 0.7 萬人，政府
部門減少 1.7 萬人。薪資方面，私人部門平均時薪升至 37.81 美元，月增 0.1%、
年增 3.0%，平均每週工時維持 34.4 小時。另 7 月與 8 月新增就業合計下修 6 萬
人，反映前二月就業動能較初估疲弱。整體而言，9 月美國新增就業明顯放緩、
失業率小幅上升且薪資增速降溫，企業聘僱動能較先前轉弱；惟失業率仍維持於
近期區間，勞動市場尚未出現明顯惡化跡象，後續仍須關注企業聘僱需求是否持
續降溫，以及對聯準會利率決策的影響。 
2026 年 8 月美國職位空缺數(Job Openings) 由上月修正後之 733.5 萬個減少
25.6 萬個至 707.9 萬個 ， 職缺率由4.4%降至 4.3%， 顯示企業用人需求略有降溫 ；

惟聘僱人數由 514.6 萬人小幅增加至 519.2 萬人，聘僱率由 3.2%升至 3.3%。總離
職人數由 512.8 萬人減少至 507.0 萬人，離職率維持於 3.2%。就離職結構觀察，
主動離職(Quits)人數由 308.9 萬人降至 306.6 萬人，主動離職率維持 1.9%；裁員
與解僱(Layoffs and Discharges) 人數則由 170.2 萬人降至 164.1 萬人，裁員率由
1.1%降至 1.0%。產業別方面，職位空缺增加主要來自零售業、金融保險業、資訊
業及休閒餐飲業 ； 聘僱人數則以政府部門及製造業增幅較為明顯 。 整體而言 ，8 月
美國職缺數較前月下滑，反映企業徵才需求略有降溫，但聘僱活動仍維持穩定，
且主動離職與裁員規模同步下降，顯示勞工轉職意願及企業人力調整趨於審慎，
勞動市場供需仍大致維持平衡。 
2026 年 9 月美國 ISM 製造業採購經理人指數(Manufacturing PMI) 為 54.5，
較上月之 54.6 微降 0.1 點，惟已連續第九個月維持於 50 以上擴張區間，顯示製
造業景氣持續擴張。細項觀察，新訂單指數由53.7 升至 55.3，需求動能續增；生
產指數由 58.3 降至 56.7，雖仍維持擴張，但成長速度略有放緩；就業指數由51.2
升至 52.7，反映企業聘僱需求持續增加。供應商交貨指數由 59.3 微降至 59.0，交
貨延遲情況仍存；庫存指數則由 50.6 降至 48.6，企業備貨態度轉趨保守。此外，
價格指數由 71.1 升至 77.9，原物料及投入成本壓力升高；未完成訂單指數由51.8
升至 56.4，訂單去化變慢。另一方面，新出口訂單指數由53.2 降至 50.9，進口指
數亦由 52.5 降至 51.0，外需成長動能及企業備料需求略為放緩。整體而言，9 月
美國製造業活動持續穩健擴張，新訂單、就業及未完成訂單同步改善，顯示製造
業景氣仍具韌性；惟出口及進口增速放緩、投入成本壓力升高，加上關稅政策、
地緣政治風險及供應鏈不確定性仍存，後續發展仍值得持續關注。 
2026 年 8 月美國個人消費支出(PCE)物價指數月增率由上月之 0.1%升至 0.3%，
年增率維持於 3.4%； 扣除食品及能源後之核心PCE 月增率由 0.1%升至 0.2%，年
增率亦持平於 3.0%，顯示整體通膨大致持穩，惟短期物價漲勢較前月略有回升。
另一方面，個人所得月增 0.2%，可支配所得(DPI)月增 0.3%，實質可支配所得持
平；個人消費支出月增 0.9%，實質個人消費支出成長 0.6%，顯示民間消費表現
明顯回溫。由於消費增幅高於所得增幅，個人儲蓄率由前月之 4.6%降至 4.1%。
整體而言，8 月美國通膨表現大致持穩，但消費支出顯著回升，反映家庭需求仍
具韌性；後續仍須持續關注消費熱度、通膨走勢及其對聯準會貨幣政策路徑之影
響。

6

近期聯準會官員談話呈現鷹鴿分歧。聯準會理事 Michael Barr 認為，中東局
勢推升能源價格，以及 AI 相關投資熱潮帶動需求增長，均可能阻礙通膨回落至
2%目標 ， 未來仍不排除進一步升息的必要性 ； 惟 紐約聯準銀行總裁 John Williams、
聯準會副主席 Philip Jefferson 及金融監理副主席 Michelle Bowman 則強調，在 9
月升息後，當前並無急迫性立即進一步收緊政策，應先觀察後續通膨、就業及金
融市場數據變化，再評估是否有進一步調整利率的必要。受官員談話轉趨審慎，
以及 9 月非農就業表現疲弱影響，市場對 10 月 FOMC 會議再度升息的預期明顯
降溫，政策焦點逐漸轉向 12 月會議及年底前是否仍有一次升息空間。 
晶圓代工龍頭台積電傳出正評估與馬斯克旗下 Terafab 合作，在美國德州布
局晶圓廠，馬斯克也於社群平台 X 證實雙方正在討論，並表示 「目前僅在討論階
段 ， 但可能會有進展」 。 目前浮上檯面的合作模式包括由台積電持有並營運新廠 ，
SpaceX 或 Terafab 透過入股及承諾採購晶圓參與；另一方案則由 Terafab 掌握多
數股權 ， 台積電提供製程技術與晶圓廠營運經驗 。Terafab 旨在因應 Tesla、SpaceX
及 xAI 快速成長的 AI 晶片需求，首期投資規模達 168 億美元。若合作成真，不
僅可望成為台積電繼亞利桑那州後在美國的新布局，也凸顯先進製程與穩定晶片
供應的重要性，但目前具體合作方式、時程與投資內容仍未正式定案。 
二、歐元區—法國殖利率大幅上升，不利於投資及消費表現 
 歐元區 9 月 HICP 通膨 、 核心通膨(不含食品及能源)分別上升 0.6、0.1 個百分
點至 3.8%、2.5%， 主因國際原油價格上漲 ， 使能源通膨上升4.5 個百分點至 18.8%，
並透過肥料、物流成本上漲，間接推升食品通膨 0.3 個百分點至 1.4%。另外，服
務通膨上升 0.2 個百分點至 3.2%，近三個月服務通膨大致呈現橫向波動，部分反
映旅遊旺季因素影響。基於布蘭特原油持續位於每桶 90 美元以上，預期年底前
仍將對通膨帶來額外壓力，後續須關注能源漲價可能會透過運輸、物流等成本管
道傳導至核心商品及服務價格，進而提高核心通膨黏性。 
 其他經濟數據包含 ：(1)歐元區 8 月失業率持平 6.4%， 失業人數增加2.6 萬人 ，
主要是義大利勞動參與人數增加所致，不過 ，基於PMI 顯示近期德國勞動雇用需
求持續增溫，將有助於穩定勞動市場表現；(2)歐元區第二季家庭儲蓄率下降 0.2
個百分點至 14.2%，顯示通膨壓力削弱民眾購買力，致儲蓄暫時性減少；(3)歐元
區第二季房價年增率為 4%， 較第一季降0.6 個百分點 ， 主因法國中古屋面臨嚴格

環保法規，須負擔高昂翻修成本，使法國房價呈現連三季下滑所拖累。 
 近一週法國 10 年期公債殖利率升至 4.9%以上，與德國公債殖利率差距擴大
至 125 個基點以上，主要反映市場對法國主權公債可持續性疑慮升溫。儘管目前
政府計畫將 2027 年財政赤字縮限至 5%，惟政府在國會並無穩定多數席次，即使
能以行政命令強行通過預算 ， 也可能面臨倒閣風險 ；此外 ，市場對於2027 年 4 月
總統大選後，新總統能否取得國會足夠支持，推動削減赤字與控制債務的改革亦
缺乏信心。因此未來法國仍可能面臨殖利率進一步上升與財政壓力加劇的風險，
暫時不利於投資及消費信心。 
三、日本—景氣溫和復甦，但消費放緩與通膨升溫風險升高 
 8 月工業生產月減 1.7%，主要係受到該月地震及豪雨等天然災害影響，導致
部分工廠暫時停產。從細項來看，汽車業受停工影響最為明顯，產量下滑6.8%，
電子產品產量亦減少 1.9%。 惟生產機械產量連續三個月成長 ， 加上核心資本財出
貨持續增加，顯示企業投資需求仍相當強勁，有助於支撐後續工業生產回升。從
企業預期來看，製造商預期9 月產量將成長 3.2%，顯示生產活動可望迅速回溫，
在供應鏈逐步恢復、庫存回補需求持續，以及穩健的企業資本支出支撐下，預計
第三季工業生產仍可維持正成長，且成長動能可望延續至年底。 
 8 月零售月減 1.2%，雖然表現略優於市場預期，但仍顯示民間消費動能正在
放緩。此次疲弱主要原因來自耐久財銷售下滑，其中家電銷售因空調等產品在換
機需求告一段落後明顯回落，汽車銷售也隨政府刺激措施效應逐漸消退而連續第
四個月下降。相較之下，食品、服裝等非耐久財銷售雖同步走弱，但降幅相對有
限 ， 反映薪資成長仍對消費提供部分支撐 。惟不利天候與通膨壓力仍是主要風險 ，
8 月至 9 月持續豪雨已影響服裝等非必需消費，而除米價以外的食品及商品價格
持續上漲，也正侵蝕家庭實質購買力，恐限制消費者信心與消費復甦力道。 
 9 月東京核心通膨年增率升至 2.7%，雖然東京都免費托兒及免費水費政策造
成的基期效應貢獻約 0.5 個百分點，但通膨升溫主因仍是價格上漲已由能源與食
品擴散至整體經濟。受石油價格上揚帶動，企業開始加速將原物料及薪資成本轉
嫁給消費者，帶動商品與服務價格普遍走高；租金通膨更升至 2%，創 1994 年以
來新高，公共交通及醫療等項目也開始調漲。隨著東京的物價壓力逐步反映至全
國數據，日本央行進一步升息的可能性升高。

四、中國—為刺激內需、引導產業走向，人行漸近式放寬政策工具 
9 月官方製造業 PMI 上升 0.3 點至 50.1，為近三個月高點，新訂單、出口訂
單指數分別為 50.5、50，並帶動生產指數上升至 51.7，不過，庫存指數下降、就
業指數加速衰退、企業展望減弱，皆拖累 PMI 指數水準。此外，投入、產出物價
指數分別上升至 60.8、54，顯示成本壓力加劇，但售價調整速度依然較為遲緩，
不利於獲利表現。官方非製造業 PMI 上升 1.2 點至 50.2，其中建築業活動指數大
增 3.4 點至 50.3，脫離連續八個月的衰退區間，主要是政府加大財政刺激力度，
推動重大建設開工；非製造業的從業人員指數為 45.9，顯示就業情況仍顯嚴峻，
亦較不利於內需表現。 
鑒於內需動能疲弱，人行於 9 月 29 日宣布下調抵押補充貸款(該貸款本質是
將資金引導至政府希望發展的領域)利率自 1.75%降至 1.50%，並擴大適用範圍，
將水網、新型電網、算力網、新一代通信網、城市地下管網及物流網等建設納入
支持，有助於提振內需動能。未來若內需復甦力道不如預期，不排除人行將進一
步降準或擴大結構性貨幣工具支持經濟；至於全面降息則仍須兼顧人民幣匯率及
中美利差等因素，可能性相對較低。 
五、印度及東南亞—泰國、菲律賓出口均達 2 成以上的高速成長

條件不佳影響，預期新加坡進口食品、商品及服務成本將持續上升，惟國內生產
率成長及政府補貼政策將抑制部分服務業通膨，新加坡金管局(MAS)或在 10 月
「略微」緊縮貨幣政策。(2)泰國：8 月出口額年增 24.3%(346 億美元)，主要由工

運輸設備（13.6%）為主。進出口相抵，8 月貿易逆差 24.8 億美元，較 7 月略收

2 群益投顧 
  【 投 資 評 等 說 明 】 
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy)  5%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無 法 由 基 本 面 給 予 投 資 評 等 
預 期 近 期 股 價 將 處 於 盤 整 
建 議 降 低 持 股

【 投 資 評 等 調 整 機 制 】 
  投 資 評 等 係「 首 次 」給 予 特 定 個 股 投 資 評 等 時，其 前 一 日 收 盤 價 相 較3 個月
目 標 價 之 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 計 算 而 得 。 個 股 投 資 評 等 分 為四 個 等 級 ， 定 義 如 上 。
爾 後 的 投 資 評 等係 依 循 「 首 次 評 等 」 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。  
「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」及 「 區 間 操 作 」均有 upside 目 標 價 。差 別 僅 在 於 ，
首 次 評 等 時 潛 在 報 酬 率 不 同 。 「 中 立 」 則 無 目 標 價 。 
一 旦 我 們 給 予 特 定 個 股「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」或「 區 間 操 作 」之 投 資 評 等，
就 是 責 任 的 開 始，爾 後 將 透 過 各 式 研 究 報 告 作 定 期 性、持 續 性 追 蹤 基 本 面 及
股 價 變 化 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。 
停 止 推 薦 情 境 ： 
1.  達 目 標 價 。 
2.  雖 未 達 原 訂 目 標 價 ， 但 檢 視 基 本 面 、 訊 息 面 、 籌 碼 面 等 多 方 訊 息 ， 研
判 股 價 上 漲 空 間 已 然 有 限 ， 將 適 時 出 具 降 評 報 告 。 
3.  推 薦 後 股 價 不 漲 反 跌 。

【免責聲明】  
  本 研 究 報 告 僅 提 供 予 特 定 人 之 客 戶 作 為 參 考 資 料「 非 經 同 意 不 得 轉 載 」 。我
們 並 不 確 保 此 資 訊 的 完 整 性 與 正 確 性，投 資 人 應 了 解，報 告 中 有 關 未 來 預 測
之 陳 述 可 能 不 會 實 現，因 而 不 應 被 依 賴。而 且 此 報 告 並 非 根 據 特 定 投 資 目 的
或 依 預 定 對 象 之 財 務 狀 況 所 撰 寫 出 來 的，因 此，此 研 究 報 告 的 目 的，既 非 對
投 資 人 於 買 賣 證 券、選 擇 權、期 貨 或 其 他 證 券 相 關 之 衍 生 性 商 品 提 供 詢 價 服
務，亦 非 作 為 進 行 交 易 的 要 約。投 資 人 應 注 意 到 相 關 證 券 之 價 值 及 收 益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

M
Idea
Further upside surprise: In our recent CPO report, we raised WIN Semi's revenue
forecasts  to reflect stronger demand for satellite communications RF, driven by
major customer SpaceX (SPCX; covered by Adam Jonas). However, we
underestimated the favorable pricing and margin, as evidenced by the 28 Oct   3Q26
results. Reflecting the much higher gross margin, we raise our 2026, 2027 and 2028
EPS by 35%, 28% and 17%, respectively, and raise our price target 17% to  NT$700  to

capture the long-term upside from both new businesses - space communications
and AI optical networking laser.
3Q26 margin beat: Core margin reached 40.7% in 3Q26, well above our original
forecast of 29%. The company indicated space communications revenue already
represents 40% of its infrastructure business, accounting for 35-40% of total
revenue. This implies the space-related revenue is tracking at c.15% of total revenue
in 3Q. Despite a relatively  small portion of revenue, we estimate the business could

lift 3Q26 margin by 5% (half of 3Q26 increase), implying the space communications
margin could be >60%, well above our and Street expectations. V3 satellite
development will be a key growth driver into 2027. See Exhibit 1  for our analysis.
4Q26 revenue guidance looks solid: Smartphone and WiFi revenue remain stable
despite weak China smartphone demand: Win Semi now has higher exposure to
Apple iPhone through high-band PA (Broadcom) and WiFi (Murata). As such,

revenue can still grow single digit QoQ in 4Q26, despite recent weakness in China
smartphone demand. Please see Exhibit 3  and Exhibit 4    for  our earnings review.
Upgrade to OW: The stock trades at 25x our new 2028 EPS. Given the significant
revenue contribution from space communications and AI lasers, we think our
implied target multiple of 29x 2028 EPS should be justified. Win Semi historically
traded at 40x PE during the smartphone 3D sensing product cycle.

Surprising Margin Expansion from Optical/Space Businesses
WIN Semi   could benefit from PD growth and potential LD outsourcing
by late 2027
In our previous WIN Semi report, we argued that Lumentum (LITE.O, covered by Meta
Marshall) was unlikely to outsource CW lasers or EML production to WIN Semi, a key
pillar of our earlier UW rating. However, our recent  checks with WIN Semi management
suggest that WIN Semi and its major customer, we assume it's Broadcom (AVGO, covered

by Joseph Moore), have secured sufficient InP substrate supply for WIN Semi's PD
production, potentially supporting substantial growth for the PD (photo diode) business
in 2H26. We estimate PD could account for >10% of WIN Semi's total revenue. Our recent
checks from the supply chain also suggest that WIN Semi's largest PD customer  could
outsource LD (laser diode) production to WIN Semi.
We also see the potential for even stronger revenue growth from WIN Semi's aerospace

business into 2027, especially from the ground station and satellite business. With
SpaceX's  launch of Starship and Starlink V3 satellite, we expect WIN Semi's content value
per satellite   to increase significantly from Starlink v2 to v3, with potential upside from
future DTD (direct to device) satellites. The increase in content value comes not only from
higher RF chips volumes, but also from the migration from GaAs to GaN. Based on our
industry checks, the ASP for 4-inch GaN could be US$9k, compared with ~US$2.5-3k for 6-

inch GaAs. We therefore upgrade our rating from EW to OW with the strong gross margin
performance, which we believe to be >60%.
Where we could be wrong with our OW call: We think the largest near-term swing factor
for WIN Semi is the aerospace business, which is relatively difficult to track given the
inherent risk of delays or failures in satellite launches. However, on September 28, 2026,
SpaceX successfully launched the Starship with Starlink V3 satellite. If SpaceX decides to

speed up the satellite constellation, there also could be upside to our forecasts.
All of our satellite launch numbers are based on Adam Jonas's SpaceX forecasts. Please
see his latest report: SpaceX: SpaceX/Tesla and SI's Next Act (23 Oct 2026)

3Q26 earnings and 4Q26 guidance review - margin and earnings beat on

strong optical and aerospace demand
WIN Semi delivered a solid 3Q26 performance, with margins and earnings exceeding
expectations, supported by robust demand across its optical and aerospace businesses.
The gross margin beat MSe and consensus by 7-8ppts.  Strength in optical applications,
driven by continued investment in AI-related optical connectivity and high-speed data
transmission, alongside resilient aerospace demand, supported a favorable product mix

and improved manufacturing efficiency.
The higher contribution from these relatively high-value-added segments lifted gross
margins above expectations, driving positive operating leverage and stronger-than-
anticipated earnings. Looking ahead, we expect sustained momentum in optical
communications and aerospace applications to remain key drivers of WIN Semi's
profitability.

WIN Semi: Valuation Methodology
Our price target (base-case value) increases from NT$600 to NT$700: This also factors
in our EPS forecast changes  for 2025 to 2028.
We continue to derive our price target from a residual income model as we believe it best
presents the value of the company. All of our other key assumptions are unchanged,
including a cost of equity of 8.0% (beta of 1.0, equity risk premium of 6% and risk-free

rate of 2%), intermediate growth rate assumption of 15% and terminal growth rate of
4.0%.
Our bull-case value  increases  from NT$900 to NT$1050 and our bear-case value from NT
$300 to NT$350, implying 44x and 14x 2028e EPS, respectively.

▪ Relatively strong iPhone demand (vs.
Android) appears likely in the near term, but
smartphone growth is mature compared to

AI demand.
▪ We expect to see more GaAs wafer
consumption as we migrate to the WiFi 7
era, as WiFi 7 has three bands (2.4 GHz, 5
GHz and 6 GHz) vs. WiFi 6 at just two (2.4
GHz, 5 GHz).
▪ GaN penetration in satellite business could
lift the wafer ASP.
▪ We think optical transceivers and robotics
may become future drivers, as WIN Semi has
the technology to enable products like EML,
CW-DFB and DML.
▪ We are OW given the strong margin
improvement from the new business.

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

Airtac
Valuation
We derive our target price of NT$1,430 for Airtac by using a P/E methodology, as we believe the margin and earnings growth
outlooks will be the key drivers of the stock. We apply a P/E of 21.5x for 2027E, which is at par with its historical average.
Risks
Key downside risks that could impede the Airtac stock from reaching our target price include: 1) a softer-than-expected China
macro economy, resulting in downside to our sales forecasts; 2) intensifying competition leading to gross margin pressures;

and 3) slower-than-expected progress of its linear guide business, causing low utilization of capacity.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds

裝 / 測 試 稼 動 率 皆 超 過 90% ， 加 上 產 品 調 價 與 產 品 組 合 改 善 ， 4 Q26 營 收 、

SSD 需 求 增 加 ， 邏 輯 業 務 亦 維 持 雙 位 數 成 長 。 且 隨 載 板 等 材 料 成 本 上 升 ，
力 成 依 不 同 產 品 向 客 戶 反 映 成 本 ， 漲 價 幅 度 介 於 個 位 數 至 雙 位 數 ， 有 助 於
維 持 毛 利 率 改 善 趨 勢

邏輯 IC 封 測 領 域 。 主 要 產 品 為 DRAM 、 Flash 、 以 及 邏 輯 IC 封 測 。 目 前 美
光為力成的最大客戶，其次為東芝 Toshiba 、 金 士 頓 。 主 要 競 爭 對 手 為 ： 日
月 光 集 團 、 京 元 電 、 頎 邦 、 南 茂 等 全 球 前 十 大 台 灣 封 測 廠 。 3Q2 6 服 務 別 營

評等說明  
1)  強力 買進 / Str on g  Bu y ： 我 們 對 該 評 價 個 股 未 來 展 望 正 向 ， 並 預 估 於 未 來 12 個 月 投 資 期 間 ， 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 大 於 50 % 
2)  買進 / Bu y： 我 們 對 該 評 價 個 股 未 來 展 望 正 向 ， 並 預 估 於 未 來 12 個 月 投 資 期 間 ， 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 大 於 25%  
3)  逢低 買進 /Tr a d in g  Bu y ： 我 們 對 該 評 價 個 股 未 來 展 望 正 向 ， 並 預 估 於 未 來 12 個 月 投 資 期 間 ， 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 小於 25 % 
4)  未評等 / N ot Ra te d ： 至 目 前 我 們 沒 有 足 夠 基 本 資 料 判 斷 該 公 司 評 等 ； 或 此 期 間 康 和 與 該 公 司 有 特 定 交 易 ， 基 於 規 範 或 康
和 政 策 ， 限 制 予 該 公 司 評 等

研 究 性 質 ， 所 載 述 意 見 可 隨 時 予 以 更 改 或 撤 回 並 不 另 行 通 知 ， 僅 提 供 康 和 集 團 內 部 同 仁 及 客 戶 參 考 ， 文 中 所 載 資 訊 或 任 何
意 見 ， 不 構 成 ( 且 不 應 解 釋 為 ) 在 任 何 司 法 管 轄 區 內 ， 任 何 買 賣 有 價 證 劵 或 其 他 投 資 標 的 之 要 約 、 招 攬 、 邀 請 、 宣 傳 、 誘 使 ，
或 任 何 不 論 種 類 或 形 式 之 表 示 、 建 議 或 推 薦 買 賣 本 研 究 報 告 所 述 的 任 何 證 劵 。 報 告 內 容 所 提 及 之 各 項 業 務 、 財 務 等 相 關 資
訊 、 意 見 及 預 估 ， 係 取 材 自 據 信 為 可 靠 之 資 料 來 源 ， 既 不 以 明 示 或 默 示 的 方 式 ， 對 資 料 之 準 確 性 、 完 整 性 或 正 確 性 做 出 任
何 陳 述 或 保 證 ， 且 報 告 係 基 於 特 定 日 期 所 做 之 判 斷 ， 有 其 時 效 性 限 制 ， 如 有 變 更 ， 本 公 司 將 不 做 預 告 或 更 新 。 對 於 本 投 資
報 告 所 討 論 或 建 議 之 任 何 證 劵 、 投 資 標 的 ， 或 文 中 所 討 論 或 建 議 之 投 資 策 略 ， 投 資 人 於 決 策 時 應 審 慎 衡 量 本 身 風 險 ， 並 就
投 資 結 果 自 行 負 責 。

3Q26 Review: EV 판매 증가의 역풍 
 투자의견 BUY(유지) 
 목표주가 160,000원(유지) 
 현재주가 95,600원(10/30)

 동사의 3분기 실적은 매출액 31.6조원(+10.1%yoy), 영업이익 1.8조원
(+24.3%yoy, OPM 5.8%), 지배이익 1.29조원(-9.8%yoy)을 기록, 도매 판
매 증가로 인한 매출 증가에도 불구하고 인센티브/원자재 비용 증가로 인해 
컨센서스를 하회하는 실적 쇼크를 기록 
 영업이익 증감 요인: 인센티브&가격 -7,270억원, 믹스 -4,650억원, 재료비 
-3,680억원으로 총 1.56조원 비용 발생. 증가 요인은 판매량 +4,420억원, 
환율 +5,290억원, 기타비용 절감 +4,130억원으로 1.38조원 상쇄. 인센티
브로 인한 비용 증가 예상외로 컸는데 EV 판매량 증가 영향. 기말환율 하락
으로 판매보증충당금은 전년비 9,254억원 감소 
 전동화 믹스 증가: 글로벌 xEV 소매 판매 30만대(+47%yoy), 판매비중 
36.2%(+9.8%p yoy). HEV 17.2만대(+46%yoy), 텔루라이드, 스포티지 
HEV 공급 확대로 3분기 미국 HEV 판매 +118%yoy 기록. EV 12.3만대
(+75%yoy), 서유럽 EV 판매는 +163%yoy 기록 
 4분기 전망: 중동 분쟁 지속으로 원가 부담의 급격한 개선은 어려울 전망. 
연초 CID에서 제시한 연간 판매 목표는 달성 가능할 것으로 보이지만 기존 
수익성 목표를 하회할 전망. 중장기적으로 상품 경쟁력 강화, 지역별 파워트
레인 대응 전략 유지, 내부 원가 및 체질 개선으로 외부 비용 부담 상쇄를 
추진할 계획. 중동 분쟁 완화에 따른 비용 부담 해소와 HEV 비중 확대를 통
한 수익성 개선은 2027년 시점이 될 것으로 예상 
 단기 방향 전환 어려울 듯: 판매 호조에도 불구하고 저가형 EV 판매 비중 
증가함에 따라 수익성이 악화되는 모습이 3분기에도 재확인. 글로벌 수요 
변화를 감안했을 때, EV 시장 선점이 매우 중요한 것은 사실이지만 단기 수
익성 부담 커지고 있는 점 우려됨. 중국산 EV의 글로벌 확산, 중동 전쟁 장
기화에 따른 원가 부담 증대가 연말까지는 영향을 미칠 것. 동사에 대한 투
자의견 매수와 목표주가 16만원 유지함

Compliance Notice 
당사는 자료 작성일 기준으로 지난 3 개월 간 해당종목에 대해서 유가증권 발행에 참여한 적이 없습니다 
당사는 본 자료 발간일을 기준으로 해당종목의 주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다  
당사는 동 자료를 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
조사분석담당자는 자료작성일 현재 동 종목과 관련하여 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 조사분석담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다 
동 자료는 당사의 제작물로서 모든 저작권은 당사에게 있습니다 
동 자료는 당사의 동의 없이 어떠한 경우에도 어떠한 형태로든 복제, 배포, 전송, 변형, 대여할 수 없습니다 
동 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료 및 정보로부터 얻어진 것이나, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 어떠
한 경우에도 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다

투자기간 및 투자등급/투자의견 비율 
종목추천 및 업종추천 투자기간: 12개월  (추천기준일 종가대비 추천종목의 예상 목표수익률을 의미함) 당사 투자의견 비율(%) 
ㆍSTRONG BUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +50%이상 3% 
ㆍBUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +15%이상 ~ +50%미만 94% 
ㆍHOLD(중립) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%이상 ∼ +15%미만 3% 
ㆍREDUCE(매도) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%미만 0% 
(2026.09.30 기준)

ECO CHARTS | BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IS A NEW PUBLIC
ATION AIMED AT PROVIDING AN OVERVIEW OF THE DYNAMICS

RISKS ASSOCIATED WITH THE LATTER. WE WILL PUBLISH
REGULAR UPDATES TO THIS PUBLICATION ON A QUARTERLY BASIS.

Document 301: COLA comfort for older consumers_ Older households’ spending has lagged, but the 2027 Social Security cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) could help narrow the gap. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/COLA comfort for older consumers_ Older households’ spending has lagged, but the 2027 Social Security cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) could help narrow the gap. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

13034011     1

Economy 
COLA comfort for older consumers? 
27 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Older generations' spending growth has lagged other cohorts over the last year in Bank of America aggregated credit and debit 
card data, despite sizeable wealth gains. This likely partly reflects the highly uneven distribution of wealth among older 
households, limiting the extent to which rising wealth supports spending across the cohort. 
• More recently, stronger consumer spending appears to be primarily a labor-income story. However, older households are less 
exposed to accelerating wage growth. In addition, large differences in their income sources help explain the considerable 
variation in spending among this cohort. 
• The recently announced 3.6% Social Security cost-of-living adjustment could provide a meaningful boost in 2027. If inflation 
moderates as expected, benefit increases may outpace price growth, supporting older households' purchasing power and 
potentially narrowing their spending gap with other generations.

Older generations’ spending growth has lagged  
While total credit and debit card spending per household has picked up in 2026 in Bank of America internal data, a notable 
laggard has been the spending growth of older generations (Baby Boomers and Traditionalists). For around a year, discretionary 
spending growth for these older generations has been lower than that of overall households. This is in stark contrast to 2022-
2023, when they were significantly outstripping overall household spending growth (Exhibit 1).  
Exhibit 1: Discretionary spending growth has been slower among older (Baby Boomer and Traditionalist) households despite wealth effects 
Total credit and debit card spending per household on discretionary categories by older generations and overall (three-month average, % YoY)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

### Page 2

2 27 October 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

On one level, this observation seems surprising given that in our piece on the older consumer we noted that a large and growing 
proportion of net worth is held by older cohorts (read: Entering the age of the older consumer). So what’s going on? One 
explanation is that wealth is very unevenly distributed even within the older cohort. Exhibit 2 uses the latest Federal Reserve 
(2025) Survey of Consumer Finances to illustrate that, across households older than 65 years of age, around two-thirds of net 
wealth is held by the wealthiest 10% of households. As a result, wealth gains from rising stock markets may not trickle too far 
down the wealth distribution of older consumers.  
Exhibit 2: The wealth distribution for older households is very skewed  
Distribution of net wealth for households over 65 years old (%)

Source: Federal Reserve Board of Governors (2025 Survey of Consumer Finances)  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Income and spending become more diverse and unequal with age 
Another important part of the story is that the rise in overall spending growth over the past year appears to be supported by 
labor market strength as well as rising wealth. The October Consumer Checkpoint described how a pickup in after-tax wage 
growth among lower- and middle-income households was likely a key factor driving a recovery in these cohorts’ spending growth 
(read: Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers). As households age, their income sources become more diverse (Exhibit 3), 
which means older generations will likely benefit less from this pickup in wage growth.  
Exhibit 3: As households age their sources of income become more diverse  
Sources of income by age (%)

Source: Federal Reserve Board of Governors (2025 Survey of Household Economics and Decision making) 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
The greater diversity in income sources among older households helps explain the wide dispersion in their total incomes and 
spending. For example, using Bank of America internal data, Exhibit 4 shows that households over age 62 that rely solely on

Social Security have average income and discretionary spending levels close to half those of households that also draw income 
from both pensions and employment. Consistent with this, discretionary spending is distributed more unevenly among older 
generations by income than it is across all households (Exhibit 5).  
Exhibit 4: There is considerable diversity in income and spending 
across older households…

Discretionary card spending per household by income relative to lower-

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

And, interestingly, the latest Bank of America Market Landscape Insights Study shows that retirees are least confident in an 
improvement in their finances compared to those in work or unemployed over the next six months (Exhibit 6). Again, this may 
reflect the lower exposure of many retirees to the labor market, with many on relatively fixed income sources.  
Exhibit 6: Retirees expect their finances to be little changed over the next six months  
Response to a question on the state of respondent’s expectation on their finances over the next six months (% responding “much better” or “somewhat 
better”)

### Page 4

  I NSTI TUTE

The 2027 COLA may offer some respite  
Still in our view there are some reasons for optimism around older generations spending outcomes.  
For some older households in the middle of the income distribution, tax policy is likely providing some support to their spending 
growth. The One Big Beautiful Bill Act introduced a temporary deduction of up to $6,000 for each taxpayer aged 65 or older for 
2025-2028, with the benefit phasing out above $75,000 of modified adjusted gross income for single filers and $150,000 for 
joint filers. This measure is likely lifting after-tax income and spending capacity for older households – particularly those in the 
middle of the income distribution.  
However, households on lower incomes may see less benefit from these policies as they likely already owe limited federal 
income tax. For these households relying on Social Security either as their sole or as a large component of their income, the 
forthcoming annual cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) to social security benefits will likely be a more important determinant of 
their budgets for 2027. The announced COLA for 2027 is a rise of 3.6% (Exhibit 7) – the largest since 2023, though lower than 
the 8.7% rise in 2022. 
Exhibit 7: The 2027 COLA increase is the largest since 2023 and close to the historical average  
Social Security cost of living adjustments (% increase as of January effective date)

Source: US Social Security Administration (SSA)   
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
The COLA increases are based on the year-over-year (YoY) % change between the third quarters of successive years in the 
Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers (CPI-W) and are typically announced in October and effective 
the following January. If the COLA increase simply keeps benefits in line with inflation, why is it particularly interesting? For sure, 
many times it is not. But when inflation is close to a turning point, the increase can end up looking relatively high or low 
compared with inflation over the course of the following year. The 2022 COLA increase was a case in point, as inflation in the 
subsequent year slowed sharply. In our view, this was one reason older generations’ spending growth was stronger than that of 
other age cohorts over 2022-2023.   
Exhibit 8: Inflation is expected to decline next year 
Consumer Price Index (CPI) (% YoY), third quarter CPI-W increases (red dots, % YoY) and latest consensus CPI forecast for 2027 (dotted line, % YoY)

Source: Haver Analytics, Bloomberg. Some fall 2025 months might be missing due to the government shutdown.

Regarding the 2027 COLA announcement, there are some parallels with 2022, albeit on a smaller scale. In particular, the 
consensus expectation is that CPI inflation is likely to cool over 2027, largely reflecting the lessening impact of higher gasoline 
prices (Exhibit 8).  
So if these expectations prove correct, households that rely on Social Security may receive a more impactful boost to their 
budgets next year than they have for the last few, potentially narrowing the gap between older generations’ spending and other 
households.  
What categories of spending growth might benefit from this? It is hard to be sure, but notable growth deficits for older 
generations currently reside in discretionary areas like electronics, lodging and restaurants (Exhibit 9). Some of these may reflect 
the preferences of older households, but it could be that there is room for some narrowing in these areas.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Store category aggregation for “more discretionary”, “partly discretionary”, and “mostly necessity” is defined as follows: 
More discretionary: furniture, electronics, building materials, hobbies, miscellaneous stores, nonstore retail, clothing. 
Partly discretionary: auto, health and personal care, general merchandise. 
Mostly necessity: Gasoline, food and beverage.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Bank of America Proprietary Market Landscape Insights Study is an online quantitative survey among Bank of America customers and 
noncustomers sampled and balanced to provide a representative view of the U.S. adult population. Insights are based on aggregated and 
anonymized responses to surveys. Significance testing is done at the 90 percent confidence interval. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Document 306: COP31_ Why it still matters for businesses

### Page 1

Is COP still worth following? We think so. What matters for
businesses is whether this year’s COP31 can improve the
business case for action. Here are four areas to watch

Coco Zhang and Gerben Hieminga
The global climate landscape has changed signiﬁcantly since world
leaders committed 11 years ago to limiting global warming to well
below 2°C and pursuing eﬀorts to limit warming to 1.5°C.
Geopolitical tensions, policy shifts, trade protectionism, rising
energy demand… they have all made it harder for governments to
turn climate commitments into action.
That raises an important question for businesses: Is the Conference
of the Parties (COP) still worth following?
Our answer is yes.
Businesses do not just invest in climate targets; they invest in viable
projects and operations. But many of them still face economic and
practical barriers. Grid constraints and high electricity costs can
slow electriﬁcation. Greener industrial products can struggle to

attract buyers willing to pay a premium. And climate adaptation
plans do not always translate into investable projects.
Businesses cannot solve these challenges alone. In a 2025 UN
survey of more than 2,000 CEOs across 128 countries, 92% said
strong global governance and coordinated policy are “critical” or
“important” to implementing their sustainability agendas.
That is why COP is still relevant. It can help align policy priorities,
improving investment conditions, and connecting businesses with

the right resources. At COP31, four areas will be particularly
important for businesses to watch.
Electriﬁcation marries economic growth
with sustainability
Electriﬁcation is increasingly important for economic growth,
energy security, and sustainability. Demand from AI, EVs, heat
pumps and industrial decarbonisation is accelerating electricity
use, which is growing twice as fast as overall energy demand.
Electricity also punches above its weight economically, accounting

for 23% of global ﬁnal energy consumption but powering 46% of
global GDP. This implies that high value-added sectors rely more on
electricity than the overall economy.
Electricity’s economic contribution
outweighs its share of energy use

Source: ING Research, International Energy Agency
COP31’s push to accelerate electriﬁcation reﬂects this trend. The
COP31 presidency has set a goal of increasing electricity’s share of
ﬁnal energy consumption from 23% today to 35% by 2035.
Getting there will require addressing a major constraint: the grid. As
we have written, grid congestion could slow electriﬁcation, increase
costs, and delay electricity-dependent economic activity.
The key question at COP31 is how governments turn this ambition

into action. We think eﬀective action could include:
Establish a detailed electriﬁcation roadmap with interim
benchmarks to guide progress and sustain momentum across
future COPs
Enhance knowledge-sharing platforms. The International
Smart Grid Action Network has extensive experience sharing
best practices, but could go further by translating knowledge
into direct technical assistance and ready-to-deploy models
Set country- or sector-level targets and pathways to encourage

industry alignment and commitment
Improve access to ﬁnance by expanding dedicated ﬁnancing
for developing countries, while lowering upfront costs in Europe
Improve the economics. Coordinate energy pricing reforms to
make electriﬁcation more competitive with fossil fuel
combustion alternatives, including by reducing taxes and grid
fees that raise electricity prices
Strengthen infrastructure by encouraging governments to
reduce permitting barriers (e.g. Europe and the US), advance

regulatory support for power demand ﬂexibility, and expand

If COP31 delivers meaningful action in these areas, the implications
for businesses could be signiﬁcant. Stronger policy could accelerate
near-term grid connections and enhance long-term investment
conﬁdence. Meanwhile, companies may need to work more closely
with utilities and regulators to secure the power they need.
Green industrialisation remains critical

But electriﬁcation is only part of the story. Industry is another
major source of process emissions, with sectors such as steel,
cement, and aviation accounting for roughly 20% of the global
total. Cutting these emissions is particularly diﬃcult because many
industrial processes still have few cost-eﬀective alternatives without
lengthy site overhauls.
COP31 has therefore put green industrialisation high on the
agenda, building on COP30’s progress. Following the Belém

Declaration on Global Green Industrialisation at COP30, a coalition
led by the Brazilian and UK governments mobilised hundreds of
millions of dollars in concessional ﬁnance for Brazilian industries
and awarded funding to 12 hydrogen projects. Over time, these
eﬀorts could reshape manufacturing processes and investment.
But many industrial decarbonisation technologies still face
signiﬁcant barriers to scaling. Their high cost premiums mean
economics depend heavily on government support. Demand for

cleaner industrial products remains limited. And the necessary
infrastructure for many is still underdeveloped.
Hard-to-abate sectors’ green premium is
way higher than what companies are willing

COP31 is not setting a new level of ambition for green
industrialisation. It’s focusing on implementing the existing
agenda. One priority is narrowing the demand gap. COP31 can
coordinate governments to create more demand for low-carbon
industrial products through long-term public procurement. If such
eﬀorts gain momentum at COP31, they could provide the long-run
revenue certainty investors need to back green industrial projects at

scale.
Climate ﬁnance faces multiple gaps
To realise the targets and priorities set forth at COP, investment is
crucial. And total ﬂows are growing. Global climate ﬁnance reached
around $2tn in 2024 and continued to grow in 2025, although at a
slower rate.

But the headline number masks signiﬁcant gaps. First, even though
climate mitigation (i.e. emissions reduction) ﬁnance accounted for
$1.9tn of the $2tn total, the level falls well short of estimated
needs, which range from $7.6tn-$9.6tn through 2050. In areas such
as electriﬁcation and green industrialisation, funding is needed not
only for technologies, but also for the infrastructure and demand

needed to scale them.
Second, only $64bn of the total went exclusively to adaptation, an
area requiring surging investment because of intensifying climate
risks. Third, a substantial share of climate ﬁnance stays within
advanced economies or comes from domestic sources.
International ﬂows to help developing countries totalled only
$175bn, far below COP29’s target of $1.3tn per year by 2035.

More climate products and solutions are emerging in the latter two
segments. But their higher risks or weaker business cases can make
it diﬃcult to attract capital without supportive policies. Investors are
actively turning to blended ﬁnance, which uses public capital to
help de-risk projects and mobilise private investment. Yet many still
lack the data, resources and connections needed to move projects

forward. This is where COP31 can help. As we discuss in the next
section, it can help governments create more favourable
investment conditions and connect suppliers, buyers and investors.
Adaptation is becoming a business
imperative and opportunity
Business awareness of climate adaptation has grown, as many
businesses are experiencing more disruptions ﬁrsthand. Our
previous analysis also shows how drought on Europe’s Rhine River,
hurricanes in the US, and other extreme weather events are already

aﬀecting supply chains across sectors. In aggregate, physical
climate risks could cost companies in the S&P Global 1200 index
$1.2tn annually by 2050.
One barrier to corporate investment in climate adaptation is that it
is still seen primarily as a way to avoid losses. But adaptation can
also create business value. Investments in resilient assets, cooling
systems, supplier diversiﬁcation and climate-resilient materials can
improve reliability, competitiveness and long-term growth.

Research from the World Resources Institute suggests that every $1
invested in adaptation can generate up to $10 in beneﬁts. That
return could be even higher for highly climate-exposed sectors. For
Cookie settings

### Page 8

example, the World Economic Forum estimates returns of $19–$43
per dollar invested in the food and beverage sector.
COP can help turn adaptation priorities into investable projects.
Since the Paris Agreement, countries have developed National
Adaptation Plans (NAPs), but many remain high-level and lack
speciﬁc, actionable projects. Recent COP eﬀorts have sought to
change this by encouraging governments to translate their NAPs
into portfolios of investable adaptation projects.

But identifying projects does not guarantee ﬁnancing. This is where
COP31 could make a diﬀerence. Its proposed Climate
Implementation Bridge (BRIDGE) initiative could make projects
easier for investors to identify and assess by centralising
information on technical requirements, risks, costs and beneﬁts.
Expanding the initiative to more countries could give more banks,
investors and companies access to potential adaptation
investments.
For businesses, a successful COP31 could therefore help unlock

capital for adaptation projects. The beneﬁts could extend
throughout supply chains, as companies increasingly depend on
the climate resilience of their suppliers to maintain reliable
operations.
Conclusion
The most eﬀective corporate sustainability strategies support long-
term growth, but they are more eﬀective with international
coordination and strong policy support. COP can bring
governments and businesses together to provide such a
framework, even amid geopolitical tensions and concerns over

slowing progress. COP31 may not deliver a landmark global
agreement, but practical advances in these four priorities could still
have immediate relevance for businesses.

Document 345: GLOBAL MARKETS CALL_ Government Bond Yields Ease, Gold Falls

Source PDF: all report pdf/GLOBAL MARKETS CALL_ Government Bond Yields Ease, Gold Falls.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-01
Pages: 9
Status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: GLOBAL MARKETS CALL: Government Bond Yields Ease, Gold Falls
Date:    43 minutes ago
GLOBAL MARKETS CALL: Government Bond Yields Ease, Gold Falls

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
GLOBAL MARKETS CALL:
Government Bond Yields Ease,
Gold Falls
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 1 Nov 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Gaël Gaborel - OrbisTerrae / Unsplash
Investors sought safety in government bonds last week as the AI selloff
rattled stock markets worldwide. Yields eased across most of the major
bond markets. The stock market selloff has been hardest on the markets
that soared the most this year. October is ending with the US well ahead
of the rest of the world. That validates our recent shift to market weight on
the Go Global portfolio strategy. Here’s more:

I. Stock Market Performance
The Stay Home vs Go Global ratios have rebounded sharply since mid-
year, driven by US outperformance (chart). They remain below their early
2025 peaks but are heading back up.
The US MSCI’s share of the All Country World MSCI’s market cap has
risen back to 65.3% (chart). Its share of forward earnings is 54.3%. That
11-point gap is the widest since January 2025. US stocks again are
pricing in far more than their share of global earnings.

### Page 3

October’s biggest losers were this year’s AI winners. South Korea finished
the month at the bottom of the country ETF rankings, down 16.4% in
dollars (chart). Taiwan fell 10.4%. Switzerland topped the board with an
8.8% gain, followed by Mexico (4.8%). The US slipped 0.9%, while the All
Country World ex-US ETF fell 2.9%.
II. Stock Markets Earnings & Valuation

### Page 4

Overseas forward earnings momentum is the strongest in over 15 years.
Forward earnings for the All Country World ex-US MSCI is up 41.7% y/y,
topping its 2021 post-pandemic peak (chart). Forward revenues is up
11.6% y/y. Earnings outpacing revenues by that much implies forward
profit margins are widening significantly.
Japan leads the major markets in forward earnings growth since early
2009 (chart). Its forward earnings has risen 720.7% in yen. The US is up

524.6%. EM forward earnings has surged this year and is now up 224.7%
over the same period. The EMU and the UK have barely doubled.
Valuation multiples are falling even as earnings soar (chart). The US
MSCI’s forward P/E has dropped to 19.1 from 23.7 a year ago. Japan’s is

### Page 5

14.7, and the EMU’s is 13.9. The UK trades at 11.9. EM trades at 8.9, its
lowest since the 2008 financial crisis.
III. Fixed Income
Government bond yields eased last week in most major markets in
response to the AI selloff (chart). The US 10-year Treasury yield has fallen
to 4.91% from 5.00% a week earlier. France’s 10-year government bond
yield has eased to 4.98%. The UK’s 5.30% and Australia’s 5.14% remain
the highest in the group. Japan’s yield has dropped to 2.76%.

IV. Gold
We've been bullish on the gold price since it jumped to a new record high
above $2,000 per ounce in early 2024. We reckoned that buying by the

### Page 6

central banks of countries that feared the US and Europe might freeze
their forex reserves, as happened to Russia after it invaded Ukraine,
which would be bullish for gold. That was true until the war started in the
Middle East on February 28, 2026 (chart).
We aren't sure why gold hasn't bounced off of its key support levels.
Instead, it dropped back below $4,000 last week. The next support is
around $3,400. Rising interest rates may explain why gold has been so

weak if central bank buying is being swamped by selling from private
investors.
The People's Bank of China is still buying gold (chart). Chinese investors
have lost lots of money in their property and stock markets. Now, they
may be bailing out of gold.

### Page 7

Is there still a case for gold to reach a price of $10,000 per ounce by the
end of the decade? Yes, but the case is getting weaker. If the S&P 500
reaches 10,000 by the end of the decade, investors might rebalance their
portfolios by allocating more to gold (chart).
V. Focus On China & Brazil
China’s MSCI stock price index has fallen to 66.4 in dollars, down from
92.0 at its 2025 high (chart). The property bust and deflation continue to
weigh on the market. So does the rapidly aging population.

Chinese tech has missed the AI boom entirely (chart). The China
Technology ETF (CQQQ) has fallen to 37.78, 65.2% below its February
2021 record high. The Nasdaq-100 ETF (QQQ) is 9.5% below its record
high. We continue to favor EM ex-China over China.

### Page 8

In Brazil, President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva won the October 25 runoff
with nearly 52% of valid votes after trailing Senator Flávio Bolsonaro in the
first round. Investors had positioned for a Bolsonaro win, and Bolsonaro
says he will challenge the result in court. The Brazil MSCI stock price
index fell to 3,508.86 in local currency, giving back most of its post-first-
round gains (chart). It is now up just 1.0% ytd in local currency and 2.2%
in dollars.

### Page 1

LABOUR MARKET CHARTPACK

### Page 2

Westpac New Zealand Economics
2
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us
Key points – September quarter 2026
• The September quarter surveys pointed to ongoing softness in labour market conditions. The unemployment rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7%, continuing an
almost five-year run of increases. The unemployment rate is now at its highest level since September 2015.
• There were mixed messages in terms of jobs growth: the Household Labour Force Survey reported a 0.1% fall in employment, while the employer-

focused Quarterly Employment Survey suggested a modest rise. The Monthly Employment Indicator had also pointed to a small rise in jobs.
• The fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a fall in the participation rate. These co-movements essentially reversed the surprisingly large
increases that we saw in the June quarter, which we had suggested at the time were more likely due to sampling error in the survey rather than a
genuine surge.
• We generally recommend focusing on the unemployment rate, which is less vulnerable to sampling errors – and the message here is one of softness. Our

best interpretation is that, to the extent that jobs are growing, it’s not fast enough to absorb the growth in the population.
• While the economy has returned to growth over the last year or so, hiring activity remains weak. This may be an ongoing legacy of employment trends in
previous years, when firms were relatively reluctant to lay people off during the slowdown, which then potentially left them overstaffed even as activity
started to pick up again. It’s not clear how long this effect can persist, though.

• Wage growth remains muted. Although cost-of-living pressures have increased on workers, the existing degree of spare capacity in the labour market
doesn’t give them much leverage to negotiate larger pay increases.
• Overall, we judge these results to be weaker than the RBNZ expected, in particular the rise in the unemployment rate. This data supports the views of
those on the policy committee who were less convinced in October about the potential for second-round inflation pressures in the economy.

### Page 3

Westpac New Zealand Economics
3
Employment
• The number of people employed fell by 0.1% in the September quarter, according to the
Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS). Previous quarters were revised down a little,
reflecting downward revisions to the working-age population estimates. Annual employment
growth slowed from 0.9% to 0.7%.
• This was substantially softer than our forecast of a 0.5% rise for the quarter. Our forecast
was based on the Monthly Employment Indicator (MEI), a comprehensive measure that is

drawn from income tax data. While there are some differences in coverage for these two
measures, any significant divergence between the two is most likely to reflect sampling error
in the HLFS.
• Meanwhile, the employer-focused Quarterly Employment Survey (QES) recorded a 0.1% rise
in the number of filled jobs and a 0.6% rise in hours paid. (This survey is of course vulnerable
to sampling error as well.)
• We’re most inclined towards the MEI measure of employment, given the breadth of its

coverage. But our best interpretation is that, to the extent that jobs are growing, it’s not
yet fast enough to absorb the growth in the adult population – hence the further rise in the
unemployment rate.

### Page 4

Westpac New Zealand Economics
4
Labour force participation
• The surprising fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a drop in the participation rate
from 70.6% to 70.4%.
• These co-movements are effectively a reversal of the surprisingly large increases that we
saw in the June quarter, which we noted at the time were suggestive of sampling error in the
survey. Any such errors largely wash out when it comes to the unemployment rate, however,
which is why we recommend focusing on this measure.

• Setting aside the usual quarterly volatility, labour force participation remains caught
between two competing forces. One is cyclical weakness, especially among younger people,
who are finding it difficult to break into a “low-fire, low-hire” labour market. The teen
unemployment rate of around 25% is a record high going back to the start of the survey
in 1986.
• The other force is rising participation among the over-60s (who also make up a growing

share of the population). Kiwis have had a growing propensity to work beyond the NZ Super
age, as there is no penalty for doing so. It’s unclear to what extent this can be considered
an increase in the pool of available workers: older workers tend to have low mobility
between jobs, and if they find themselves out of work are more likely to leave the labour
force altogether.

### Page 5

Westpac New Zealand Economics
5
Unemployment and underutilisation
• The unemployment rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7% in the September quarter. This was the
highest rate since September 2015.
• While there are other indicators of spare capacity in the labour market, we’re not convinced
that there is additional information to draw from them this time.
• The broader underutilisation measure, which includes discouraged jobseekers and part-
time workers wanting more hours, was steady at 13.8% after having risen sharply from

12.9% last quarter. This measure is both higher (by definition) and more variable than the
unemployment rate, but tells a similar story in that it’s around an 11-year high.
• There was a marked 1.6% fall in the number of hours worked (although ‘usual’ hours were
down just 0.4%). However, we suspect this was largely driven by Matariki – a floating holiday
that won’t be captured in the seasonal adjustment.
• This may also account for the 2.5% rise in part-time employment, against a 0.7% fall in

full-time employment. Some workers may have been pushed into the part-time category
(less than 30 hours a week) simply by virtue of only working four days during the
survey week.

### Page 6

Westpac New Zealand Economics
6
Hiring
• Business confidence surveys have shown a lift in hiring intentions in recent months, as firms
have shaken off the initial shock of the Iran conflict and the surge in fuel prices.
• However, those stated intentions aren’t obviously turning into reality yet. Job advertisements
were down about 1% for the September quarter in seasonally adjusted terms, and they
remain well below pre-Covid levels. Job ads will capture ‘churn’ within existing roles as well

as new job creation, but both of these would be associated with a strong labour market.
• With the working-age population growing at the same time, the result was a modest fall in
the vacancy rate, one of the RBNZ’s highest-ranked measures of labour market tightness.
• We estimate that there are around 1.9 people unemployed for every job vacancy, up from
around 1.8 people this time last year.
• Firms have told us that they were reluctant to lay off workers during the economic downturn

in 2023-24, as they were wary of the extreme labour shortages that had developed in the
previous boom years. As a result, they may find themselves still overstaffed even as demand
has started to pick up again, and haven’t needed to return to hiring yet.

### Page 7

Westpac New Zealand Economics
7
Population and migration
• The working-age population grew by around 1.0% over the last year, compared to a peak of
3.3% growth over 2023. Jobs growth (based on the less volatile MEI measure) is still lagging
behind population growth on an annual basis.
• The pool of potential workers is generally becoming older, with the Baby Boomers
increasingly reaching pension age and beyond, and with migrant arrivals tending to be an

older cohort than we saw pre-Covid – more in their 30s and 40s than their 20s.
• Net migration flows have started to pick up from their lows since late 2025. Departures of
New Zealanders have come down from their highs, though they remain above pre-Covid
levels, as the relative appeal of the Australian jobs market has narrowed.
• Foreign arrivals have picked up to some degree, although the growth appears to have been
mostly driven by students rather than those on work visas.

### Page 8

Westpac New Zealand Economics
8
Sectoral and regional trends
• Jobs growth has been relatively strong in government-connected sectors such as healthcare
and education, as well as in local and central government administration.
• Mining and utilities have seen strong growth in percentage terms, but neither of these
sectors are big employers in absolute terms.
• The construction sector is now starting to see a lift in employment, after a steep decline

over 2024-25. Residential building consents have risen strongly over the last year or so,
pointing to a growing pipeline of work for the homebuilding industry.
• Sectors that are connected to international tourism, such as transport and recreational
services, had previously seen strong employment growth, but this appears to have flattened
out in recent months.
• Employment trends have generally been more positive in the South Island than the North,

which likely reflects the greater influence of the agricultural sector – while not a large
employer in itself, farmers have benefited from high export prices and their spending has
flowed into other sectors.

### Page 9

Westpac New Zealand Economics
9
Wages
• The Labour Cost Index (LCI) rose by 0.5% in the September quarter. Annual growth
remained around the lowest since 2021.
• While the overall result was in line with our forecast, the details were a little weaker than
we expected, with less contribution from private sector wages (up 0.5%) and a surprisingly
large 0.7% increase in the public sector.
• The distribution of pay rates has returned to pre-Covid trends. Only 44% of roles saw a

pay rise over the last year; of those roles that did see an increase, the average size has
converged on the 2-3% range.
• The LCI is not a measure of what workers actually receive – it reflects the cost to businesses
for a given unit of labour – and hence shouldn’t be compared to the inflation rate. A more
suitable measure is the analytical unadjusted LCI, which includes pay rises that are related
to productivity gains. This measure slowed from 3.1% to 2.9% on an annual basis, the lowest

since March 2021.
• There was a marked pickup in the proportion of firms reporting cost-of-living adjustments as
the main reason for pay increases. However, the size of those increases varied greatly, with
many of them below the rate of CPI inflation.
• This seems to reflect a shift in the justification for pay rises, rather than an overall increase in
wage pressures – firms are more likely to cite cost-of-living adjustments because they’re less
likely to cite demand-side pressures.

### Page 10

Westpac New Zealand Economics
10
International comparisons
• New Zealand’s unemployment rate is the 18th lowest in the OECD (out of 38 countries). This
ranking has been broadly unchanged over the last year or so.
• Notably, New Zealand’s unemployment rate is substantially above Australia’s, whereas
historically it has been lower on average. This shift in relative job opportunities has been a
key attractor for New Zealanders over the last couple of years – as it was in the early 2010s

when the Australian economy was benefiting from a boom in mining construction.
• However, that gap between unemployment rates is no longer widening, with the Australian
jobs market starting to soften this year as the RBA has raised interest rates to rein in inflation
(which was already running too high before the Iran conflict and the surge in oil prices). We
expect Australia’s unemployment to rise further over the year ahead.
• New Zealand has the 7th highest employment rate in the OECD (down from 6th last quarter).

We tend to have high rates of labour force participation for 15-64 year olds, and particularly
high participation for the over-65s, as the lack of means testing for NZ Superannuation
means there is no financial disincentive to working beyond pension age.

### Page 1

Paris, 23 October 2026

EcoFlash

Q3 Growth in advanced economies: solid despite shocks

Growth looks to have held up in Q3 despite successive shocks (see our analysis), as it did in 2025 and H1 2026. A longer 
Middle East conflict has fed into more persistent inflation; drought has worsened in many countries, and interest rates 
have risen further – at the short end, with both the ECB and the Fed raising policy rates in September, and even more at 
the long end. However, our nowcasts show that growth is bending without breaking in most advanced economies. GDP 
figures (due on 29 October in the US and 30 October for the euro area and its member states) are expected to confirm 
that. In the Euro area, momentum looks stable (excluding Ireland, whose GDP is volatile). In Germany, growth has been 
uninterrupted since Q4 2025, while France is likely to have returned to growth after stagnating in H1. In the US, growth 
is likely to have gathered pace, according to the GDP Now, still driven by AI, but increasingly by other sectors as well.

Euro area: resilience beneath the surface. Our nowcast points to growth slowing to 0.3% in q/q, though the pace is stable 
excluding Ireland (Irish GDP is volatile and had markedly boosted the Q2 figure). Excluding Ireland, growth should hold at its H2 
average, a sign of the bloc’s underlying resilience. Also, momentum has broadened to a growing number of sectors ( see our 
analysis). While industrial production stalled over the summer, services remained buoyant. The services PMI rebounded sharply 
in Q3 and sits firmly in expansion territory (53.0 in September). Improving job-related survey data (PMI and European Commission 
surveys) and a stable unemployment rate (6.4% in August) hint at more hiring in Q3 – a support for household consumption that 
partly offsets purchasing-power losses from persistent inflation. Corporate investment is underpinned by buoyant new investment 
lending (8.7% y/y In August, 12-month cumulative). 
France: growing, nevertheless. The succession of shocks does not appear to have prevented France from returning to 
growth in Q3, at 0.2% q/q, according to our nowcast. One-off factors accounted for the stall in activity in H1. In Q3, the drought 
worsened and industrial production remained subdued. However, market services production rebounded in July and August, 
notably in business services, restaurants, digital and real estate. The latter points to residential investment recovering after a weak 
H1. Corporate investment likely followed, as reflected in buoyant credit flows and AI-related investment. Household consumption 
is likely to have improved slightly on higher electric vehicle sales.  
Euro area  France

Source: Refinitiv, BNP Paribas  Source: Refinitiv, BNP Paribas

Germany: growth still solid. The H1 rebound in activity is moderating but continues. Our nowcast points to a fourth straight 
quarter of growth in Q3, at 0.2% q/q (after 0.3% in Q4 2025, 0.4% in Q1 2026, and 0.3% in Q2). Manufacturing activity eased 
slightly, according to the September PMI survey (53.9, down 0.4 m/m). Government spending will remain growth-supportive, with 
defence and infrastructure outlays gaining traction. Infrastructure spending continues to underpin activity in public works, which 
stands at its highest since November 2021 (2.5% 3m/3m in August). Exports (4.4% y/y between January and August) remain the 
main growth engine, helped by a pivot towards Europe (7.2%). 
United States: an expansion undeterred by inflation. Growth is likely to have gathered pace in Q3. The Atlanta Fed’s GDP 
Now points to a 3.5% annualized growth rate (AR), close to our own 3.7% forecast (0.9% non -AR) and up from 2.2% in 
Q2. Domestic demand remains the engine. Consumption is weathering the pickup in inflation, while the AI-driven capex cycle 
remains robust – as reflected in retail sales and nondefence capital-goods orders (excl. aircraft). As a result, growth in final sales

### Page 2

to private domestic purchasers 1 should print above 4% AR again. By contrast, external trade should remain a drag on growth, 
reflecting AI-related import needs. Survey data point to mome ntum that is both solid and broad -based. The ISM indices have 
remained firmly in growth territory (54.9 quarterly average for manufacturing and services alike). At the same time, they highlight 
persistent price and supply-chain pressures.

Germany   United States

Source: Refinitiv, BNP Paribas   Source: Refinitiv, Atlanta Fed, BNP Paribas

《天然氣價格：美持平或略低於去年 ，歐洲偏上行 》
美國鑽井活動緩升 ，增加較多在天然氣 ，隨美國新
的液化天然氣出口工廠上線 ，逐漸改善管道瓶頸 ，
天然氣 10月庫存僅高於五年均值 2%，注入期可持
續增至11月中，預估今年冬天暖氣度日數將持平去
年 ， 並 比 過 去 五 個 冬 季 的 均 值 低1% ， 預估美國
4Q26天然氣價格將因季節性走升，但持平或略低於
去年。卡達能源LNG船已出口 ，但非往歐洲 ，歐洲
儲氣量雖拉升 ，但僅達 70%，遠低於過往 ，TTF價
格須優於亞洲以吸引運往亞洲的 LNG轉往歐洲 ，加

上中東地緣緊張，預期4Q26 TTF價格仍有上行風險。
重點摘要
• 天然氣：美國天然氣出口增
加，庫存增速下降 ，使4Q26
美國天然氣價格持平或略低
於去年同期；歐洲因庫存偏
低的回補需求將使天然氣價
格偏上行。
• 釋儲及美俄供應柴油協議雖
對全球柴油供給影響有限 ，
但超額柴油利潤恐見頂。
• 預期綜合油企 、煉油廠雖因
汽油淡季使 4Q26 獲 利 低 於
3Q26，但獲利年比仍大幅成
長，維持股東回報 ，較看好
綜合油企。

《釋儲及放寬制裁增加柴油恐使煉油利潤見頂》
受歐美老舊煉油廠關廠 ，煉油產能因俄烏 、中東戰
事受損，估計全球煉油廠開工率年減近 800萬桶/日，
加上中、俄禁止柴油出口 ，使柴油供應比原油更緊
張。美國柴油庫存低於五年區間下緣 ，柴油價格創
高，因美未禁止柴油出口 ，柴油利潤保持強勁 。但
美協調G7國家釋出柴油儲備為期四個月 ，達成美俄

柴油供應協議增加供給 ，雖粗略換算為短期每日供
給增加不到全球 1%，預期對柴油價格壓力影響僅
5%內，明年隨設備漸修復、地緣風險降低後，才緩
降，但美國超額柴油利潤恐見頂。

獲利季比下滑 ，但年比仍大幅成長 ，現金流仍強大 ，
維持資本紀律及股東回報 。雖成品油供應仍較原油
吃緊，但煉油商超額利潤恐見頂 ，較看好綜合油企 。

《預期金價短期不利因素多，但中期緩升》
近月金價歷經 美國通膨及經濟成長 ，美國FED採取
預防管理式升息一碼 ，認為年底前仍有升息一碼空
間，但受就業降溫及通膨低於預期 ，市場降低10月
升息預期 ，加上法國國債被拋售蔓延全球債市使美
長期公債殖利率及美元走強 、黃金淨多頭部位下降 ，
壓低本月金價下跌 ，也跌破季線 。預期短線金價受
債券殖利率飆升 、實質利率走升 及美元走強 相對不
利金價走勢 ，震盪放大。但若美債收益率回落 、擔
憂債務可持續性 、分散交易對手風險及多元化資產
配置，中國人行連續23個月購金，並加大購金步伐 ，
3Q26黃金ETF呈現創紀錄淨買超 ，預期3800-4000
美元有撐，維持金價中期緩步上行。

Document 501: ここが知りたい『投資信託販売チャネルの変化と現在地』

Source PDF: all report pdf/ここが知りたい『投資信託販売チャネルの変化と現在地』.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 1

### Page 1

投資信託販売チャネルの変化と現在地

資産運用調査部　主席研究員　大木 剛（おおき つよし）
　個人の資産運用において、 投資信託はNISAによる資
産形成やリタイアメン ト資金の運用などで、 重要性を増し
ている。 投資信託の販売では、 1998年12月の銀行窓販
解禁から四半世紀以上が経過した。 ここでは、 1999年以
降の公募投資信託の販売チャネル別の純資産額 （販売 ・
解約 ・ 時価変動が反映） と構成比率の推移を資料 １ で確認
したい。
　銀行窓販の解禁から1年後の1999年末時点で、 証券
は46.4兆円 （比率90％） 、 銀行等は2.8兆円 （同5％） とな
り、 その後も銀行等は預金取引等を背景とする顧客基盤
を活かし、 2007年末には34.0兆円 （同43％） まで比率
を高めた。 一方、 証券は45.3兆円 （同57％） と比率が低
下した。
　もっ とも、 銀行等の比率は2007～2008年をピークに
低下し、 証券の比率は上昇に転じた。 証券の純資産額に
は機関投資家も保有するETF （上場投資信託） が含まれ、
2010年代以降は日本銀行による大量買入れも統計上の
証券比率を大きく押し上げた。 資料 １ ・ ２ では、 個人向け投
資信託の動きをみるためETFを除いており、 それでも
2026年8月末時点では、 証券は159.9兆円 （同70％） 、 銀
行等は67.2兆円 （同29％） となっている。
投資信託の銀行窓販は証券にどれだけ迫ったか
　2009年以降の証券の比率上昇には、 証券各社の取組
みに加え、 いくつかの要因が考えられる。
　まず、 近年はNISAなどを活用した投資信託積立の拡大
により、 資産形成層を中心に大手オンライン証券会社の
利用が大きく増えている。
　また、 メガバンク等では、 顧客層に応じたグループ内の
銀行と証券の役割分担を強化し、 銀行顧客の富裕層や資
産形成層にもニーズに応じて、 証券がサービスを提供す
る動きが寄与している。
　さらに地域銀行において、 銀行の個人向け投資信託事
業を大手証券等との包括提携の下で共同運営する動き
も、 証券比率の上昇に作用している。
　一方で、 銀行等の純資産額も近年は堅調に伸びてお
り、 比率低下は、 銀行の落ち込みによるものではなく、 証
券の伸びが上回った影響が大きい。
　もっ とも、 「銀行と証券のどちらが高成長か」 という比較
より、 顧客ニーズに応じた役割分担の重要性が増してい
る。 2026年7月に政府が策定した 「金融戦略」 も、 インベ
ス トメン トチェーンの機能強化を掲げている。 多様な資産
運用ニーズに各金融サービスの担い手が強みを活かし
て応えることが、 資産運用立国の深化につながろう。
資産運用立国の深化に求められる取組み

Document 517: コモディティ・レポート（2026年7～9月）

Source PDF: all report pdf/コモディティ・レポート（2026年7～9月）.pdf

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 20 日

調査レポート

コモディティ・レポート（2026 年 7～9 月）

調査部 主任研究員 芥田 知至  
1. コモディティ市況全般：原油が上昇傾向、銅は最高値更新、金は上値重い 
ドル建て国際商品市況全般の動向を示す FTSE・コアコモディティ CRB 指数は、2023 年 5 月 31 日に 2022
年 1 月以来の安値をつけた後、2026 年 3～9 月には大幅に上昇して 9 月 10 日に 2008 年 7 月以来の高値を
つけた。その後、10 月にかけてやや下落した。コモディティ相場は、中東情勢や米金融政策の行方を巡って、引
き続き、不安定な推移が見こまれる。

2. エネルギー市況：足元はブレントが一時 110ドル、WTI が 100 ドル前後 
国際指標のブレント原油は、2026 年 4 月 30 日に 126.41 ドルと 2022 年 3 月以来の高値をつけた。足元は
110 ドル前後で不安定な動きとなっている。米国とイランは戦闘終結やホルムズ海峡再開に向けた動きは停滞し
たままであり、緊迫する中東情勢を受けて原油は高止まりが見こまれる。

3. ベースメタル市況：銅は 9 月上旬に 14,875 ドルの史上最高値 
銅相場は、2026 年 9 月 10 日に 14,875 ドルと史上最高値を記録し、足元は 14,000 ドル台半ばを中心に推
移している。銅は、原油高や景気悪化の影響が懸念される一方で、 AI・エネルギー転換・防衛の関連分野を中
心に需要が伸びるとの見方が根強く、高値圏での一進一退が見こまれる。

4. 貴金属市況：金は 8 月中旬に 4,700 ドル近くまで持ち直すも足元は 4,100 ドル前後 
金は、2026 年 1 月 29 日に 5,594.82 ドルと史上最高値を記録した後、6 月 30 日には 3,942.99 ドルまで下
落し、足元は 4,100 ドル前後を中心に推移している。米利上げが続くとの観測が有力な中、中東情勢も安定化に
はまだ遠く、原油高が意識されて、金は買われにくい状況が続きやすいとみられる。
